## p_3643069 : 2025-06-23_screening_strategy_and_test_per-formance_synthesisassessment_of_the_national_newborn_-_screening_programme_for_permanent_hea

RECOMMEND PUBLIC HEALTH STRATEGIES
 ARGUMENT
Assessment of the
national newborn
screening
programme for
permanent hearing
loss.
Screening strategy and test per-
formance
Synt
hesis
  19 juin 2025

HAS • Assessment of the national newborn screening programme for permanent hearing loss.  • juin 2025 2
Synthesis
Newborn hearing loss is
defined by
an
increase
in the hearing threshold that may range from
moderate hearing loss to profound deafness. If not detected and treated
promptly
, this deficit
can have major consequences for language acquisition, cognitive development and social in-
tegration, with significant economic repercussions.
To
minimise
these impacts, a national newborn hearing screening
program
me
was
launched
in France in 2014. The programme aims to identify
cases of
permanent bilateral hearing loss
(PBHL)
in newborns
with a hearing threshold ≥ 40
dB, in
accordance
with the
recommendations of the French National Authority for Health (HAS). The
objective
is
to
lower
the age at diagnosis so that appropriate care can be provided from the very first months of life.
Disparities and heterogeneity in screening
practices
at
the
regional level have also been
observed.
In this context
, the French Ministry of Health (DGS) and the French Directorate
General of Health Care
Provision (DGOS) consulted the HAS in 2022 to
evaluate
the
current
programme and formulate recommendations
to
harmonise
practices
and
enhance
the pro-
gramme’s effectiveness.
The main objective of this assessment is to establish a framework for
enhancing
the national
newborn screening programme for permanent hearing loss.
The assessment is based on a review of the literature covering the diagnostic performance of
tests, screening protocols, economic evaluations and acceptability studies. It also
includes
the
opinions of experts from various fields, including epidemiology, public health, ENT
physicians
,
psychology, ethics and health economics. Stakeholders were also consulted as part of this
assessment.
Based on data from the systematic literature review
and the working group’s deliberations, the
HAS took into consideration the following elements:
Context of newborn permanent bilateral hearing loss (PBHL)
in France
Since the order
of 23
April 2014, screening for
PBHL
has been
part of a national public health
programme aimed at
identifying
cases of hearing loss of 40
dB or more
to en
sure
early diag-
nosis. In 2019,
the
French public health a
gency
Santé publique France
(
SPF) identified signif-
icant regional heterogeneity in professional practices, resulting in
inequalities in access to care.
Currently available data on the newborn hearing screening programme:
Epidemiology and newborn screening programme for PBHL
The global prevalence of uni
-
and bilateral hearing impairment with hearing loss of more than
40
dB is estimated to be 2.21‰ [1.71 to 2.8].
In France, the prevalence of newborn PBHL in
2016 was 0.8‰ [0.8 to 1.0].
Furthermore
, an increase in screening coverage was observed in
2016, rising
from 88
% in 2015
to 96.1%
. The large
-
scale
implementation
of a catch
-
up
procedure for newborns discharged
from
healthcare facilit
ies
before
being
screen
ed
led to an
increase in its
adoption
in France,
rising
from 91.5% of facilities in 2015 to 97.8% in 2016.
Screening for hearing impairment is
conducted
using two tests:
OAE (otoacoustic emissions
(OAE
) and automated auditory brainstem response (AABR). A heterogeneity in the number
and type of tests performed, as well as in diagnostic care (or management)
,
has been
observed.

HAS • Assessment of the national newborn screening programme for permanent hearing loss.  • juin 2025 3
Performance of OAE and AABR screening tests
Regarding
OAE tests:
The sensitivity of OAE tests is estimated to be
77% [65 to 86] according
to a systematic review. Five subsequent studies indicate a variation between 70% [62.5 to
77.5] and 82.5% [76.3 to 88.7]. The specificity of OAE tests is estimated
to be
93% [92 to 93]
according to a systematic review.
In the five studies conducted subsequent to this review,
the
values range between 57.1% [49 to 65.2] and 99.3% [97.9 to 100]
.
Regarding
to AABR tests:
The sensitivity of AABR tests is estimated to be 93% [87 to 96]
according to a systematic review. The five studies conducted subsequently indicate a variation
between 91.7% [87.2 to 96.2] and 93.7% [69.7 to 99.8]. The specificity of AABR tests is
estimated
to be
97% [96 to 98] according to a systematic review, with variations from 92.1%
[87.7 to 96.5] to 96.5% [92.1 to 98.8] in the five studies conducted subsequent to this review.
Screening tests
performed
within the first 48 hours of a newborn's life have the highest referral
rate
s
,
regardless
of the test used. The performance
of OAE or AABR tests
depends
on the
equipment
employed
.
The
choice
of equipment
and its maintenance for screening can also
influence
the test results.
Currently
, no calibration guide
exists
for newborn hearing screening devices,
nor
is
standardi-
sation of
the
devices
.
The
analysed data
suggest that the sensitivity of AABR tests is greater than that of OAE tests
in newborns,
regardless of whether
they have risk factors
, whereas
t
he specificities of the
two
tests are similar.
The
expert opinion (working group
)
states that
the
test
choice
depends
mainly
on
available
equipment
,
tester training
,
and the ratio between the number of births and the number of
testers in each maternity unit (the time required to perform the OAE
test
could be
comparable
to the time required to perform the AABR test).
A threshold of 35 dB is
deemed appropriate
.
AABR tests
are
recommended to detect hearing impairment in newborns in neonatal units
and/or with risk factors. Due to their
comparable
performance,
both
tests can be used in
maternity units in newborns without risk factors. OAE tests are
faster,
and
most
maternity units
already have
the necessary
equipment
. However, several regions in France
(
such as
Île
-
de
-
France and Auvergne
-
Rhône
-
Alpes
)
have introduced screening using AABR from birth.
The performance of screening test sequences.
The sequence using OAE tests only, based on two tests, has a sensitivity ranging from 88.9%
to 100%
, and a specificity ranging from 92% to 96.8%
according to three studies
. In Spain, a
two
-
OAE test protocol demonstrates a referral rate of 3.8% and coverage of more than 95
%.
In Sweden, an OAE test followed by OAE for inconclusive
(“refer”)
results achieves an optimum
sensitivity with coverage of 99.1%. These tests are more commonly used in screening
programmes due to their simplicity, speed and low cost.
Regarding an OAE test followed by an AABR test, the sensitivity can be as high as 100%,
according to three studies. However, it is slightly lower (92.3%) according to two others. The
specificity ranges from 95.6% to 99.9%
based on
four studies. In China,
the inclusion of the
AABR test following an inconclusive OAE test has reduced the referral rate to 3%,
compared
to 11% for the OAE test alone. In Italy, a protocol combining OAE and AABR has a sensitivity
of 100% and a specificity of 99.3%, with a very low
f
alse positive
rate
(
0.03%
)
. The combination

HAS • Assessment of the national newborn screening programme for permanent hearing loss.  • juin 2025 4
of OAE and AABR provides the best overall results in the screening programme in terms of
sensitivity, specificity and predictive values, offering a more effective strategy.
The type of hearing impairment to be screened for
According to the SpF report,
heterogeneity in the type of hearing impairment is observed
across different
regions
in Fr
ance
. A descriptive study conducted in the Haute
-
Normandie re-
gion observed that the number of diagnostic assessments increased by 87%
when unilateral
hearing loss was included in the screening programme.
There are currently no data on the clinical utility and impact of implementing a screening pro-
gramme combining bilateral and unilateral hearing loss compared with screening
for bilateral
hearing loss
alone
.
According to expert opinion, screening for bilateral hearing loss is considered
a priority
to avoid
delays in diagnosis and care.
Economic evaluation of newborn PBHL screening
A study conducted in
Taiwan demonstrated that including AABR tests in a newborn hearing
screening programme increased the cost
-
effectiveness, while maintaining a low
Incremental
Cost
-
Effectiveness Ratio
(
ICER
)
.
A French study conducted in 2010 indicated that the ICER
of AABR+AABR screening compared to OAE+AABR is €118,257 per case detected, which is
high.
It is crucial to gather current French data on the indirect costs of hearing loss
to
strengthen the
available evidence.
Organisation of the newborn PBHL screening programme
According to expert opinion (working group), independent national representation of the play-
ers involved in hearing screening is essential to guarantee the monitoring, evaluation and im-
pact of the programme. However, the members of the working group were n
ot in favour of
combining newborn blood spot screening and hearing screening.
The
newborn
s
creening
pro-
cess
is limited by a lack of human resources and regional disparities in
access to services
,
as
well as
communication of results to families.
It is
essential
to provide
complete
and appropriate information to future parents during
preg-
nancy about newborn hearing screening.
International
recommendations on newborn PBHL screening programmes
Based on international guidelines, the JCIH
(Joint Committee o
n Infant Hearing)
recommends
the application of the
1
-
3
-
6 principle. According to this principle, screening should be carried
out before the first month of life, diagnostic confirmation should be obtained before three
months of age, and intervention should be carried out a
t no later than six months of age. The
OA
E
and AABR tests are recommended as the
initial screening tests in most
countries. In
2018, the European guideline recommended only AABR tests as T1 and T2.
Additionally,
most
countries recommend AABR as the second screening test.
For newborns in neonatal units, the AABR test is recommended due to an increased risk of
auditory neuropathy.

HAS • Assessment of the national newborn screening programme for permanent hearing loss.  • juin 2025 5
HAS recommendations
The HAS recommends that national newborn hearing screening be performed:
‒
In both ears.
‒
At a threshold of 35
dB
,
incorporating two phases. The first phase
includes two
tests
(T1, T2) performed
either in
the maternity or neonatal unit and the second phase (T3),
if required, performed in the first month following birth.
‒
Before a corrected age of 1 month in preterm infants.
‒
With the
consent of the parents or holders of parental authority
,
where applicable, ob-
tained after
provid
ing
t
hem
with
complete
and accessible information.
‒
For infants who have not undergone hearing screening or who were born in an atypical
context (e.g. early discharges, home births), the HAS recommends
conducting
a catch
-
up phase.
First screening phase (Test 1 and Test 2)
For the first screening phase, the HAS recommends
conducting two
screening tests (or
only
one, if the first is conclusive
or “pass"
). The choice of the number and type of tests to be carried
out is based on an assessment of the following criteria
(Fig.1)
:
‒
Births in a maternity unit
‒
Newborn in a neonatal unit
‒
Duration of
stay in a
maternity unit
‒
Presence of risk factors in addition to prematurity and transfer to a neonatal unit, as
shown in the table below:
Table
1
.
Main risk factors for newborn hearing impairment.
(1)
Risk factors
Perinatal
1.
Family history of early, progressive, or delayed onset
of
permanent
childhood hearing loss
2.
Neonatal intensive care
for
more than 5 days
3.
Hyperbilirubin
emia with exchange transfusion regardless of length of stay
4.
Aminogl
ycoside administration for more
than 5 days
b
5.
Asphyxia or hypoxic
I
schaemic
E
ncephalopathy
6.
Extracorporeal membrane oxygenation
(ECMO)
a
7.
In utero infection
s
with cytomegalovirus (CMV) or early congenital infection with CMV
c
.
In utero infections, such as herpes, rubella
d
, syphilis, and toxoplasmosis
Mother + Zika
and infant with laboratory eviden
ce and/or no clinical findings
8.
Certain birth conditions or findings:
Craniofacial malformations including
microt
ia/atresia, ear dysplasia,
oral
facial clefting, Waardeburg syndrome, and microphthalmia; Congenital microcephaly, congenital or ac-
quired hydrocephalus; Temporal bone abnormalities
9.
Over 400 syndromes have be
en identified
with atypical hearing thresholds
e

HAS • Assessment of the national newborn screening programme for permanent hearing loss.  • juin 2025 6
Risk factors
Perinatal or postnatal
10.
Culture
-
positive infections associated with sensorine
ural hearing loss, including
confirmed bacterial and viral
(especially herpes viruses and varicella) meningitis or encephalitis
11.
Events associated with hearing loss:
Significant head trauma
,
especially basal skull/temporal bone frac-
tures
Chemotherapy
a.  Infants at increased risk of delayed onset or progressive hearing loss.
b. Infants with toxic levels or with a known genetic susceptibility remain at risk.
c. In all newborns in whom foetal ultrasound monitoring has demonstrated suspect ultrasound images (including in mothers
seropositive to CMV at the start of pregnancy); in all infants of mothers with documented primary infection with CMV during
pregnancy.
d. Suspected rubella infection in the absence of a history of maternal vaccination or previous infection.
e. Van Camp & Smith, 2016
For births in a maternity unit in newborns without risk factors (without transfer to the
neonatal unit).
‒
The HAS recommends performing
an initial
hearing test (T1), ideally 48 hours after
birth, favouring AABR tests.
Until this practice is widely implemented
,
along with
pro-
fessional training
and access to appropriate equipment in healthcare facilities, the first
test (T1) can
be conducted
using an OAE test.
‒
If the result of this
initial
test is inconclusive
(
“
refer
”
)
,
the HAS recommends performing
a second test (T2) by AABR before discharge from the maternity unit. If it
is not possible
to perform this second test,
due to early discharge (less than 48 hours), the HAS rec-
ommends performing a catch
-
up test (see catch
-
up phase).
For births in a maternity unit
and
newborns with risk factors (without transfer to the
neonatal unit).
‒
The HAS recommends
conducting
a first hearing test (T1) by AABR, ideally 48 hours
after birth.
‒
If this first test is inconclusive
(“refer”)
, the HAS recommends performing a second test
(T2) by AABR before discharge
.
For newborns transferred to a neonatal unit
.
‒
The HAS recommends screening
exclusively
using AABR tests
.
‒
The HAS recommends performing a first test (T1) by AABR
as close as possible to the
corrected
age
in
case
s
of prematur
ity
, and at a minimum of 36 weeks’
gestation.
‒
If the result of this first test is inconclusive
(“refer”)
, the HAS recommends
conducting
a
second test (T2) by AABR before discharge from the unit.
In all the above cases, if T2 is conclusive
(“pass”)
, the screening process
concludes
.
Second screening phase (Test 3)
The HAS recommends
organising a second screening phase (T3) if
the
T2
conducted
in the
maternity or neonatal unit (the first screening phase)
is inconclusive
(“refer”)
. For this purpose,
an appointment should be scheduled before the patient leaves the health
care
facility
to ensure
the continuity of the screening process
(Fig.2)
.

HAS • Assessment of the national newborn screening programme for permanent hearing loss.  • juin 2025 7
The HAS recommends performing a T3 by AABR at a corrected
age of one month, at the latest.
Screening can be performed in a community or hospital setting
by different professionals (ENT
physician, paediatrician,
physician at a
mother
-
and
-
baby clinic
, midwife, childcare auxiliary or
state
-
registered nurse or paediatric nurse)
,
listed by the regional operator.
Catch
-
up test
For newborns who have not undergone screening or
were born in an atypical context (e.g.
home births, birth centres, early discharges
within
48 hours), the HAS recommends
performing
a single catch
-
up AABR test. The result
of this test is taken into consideration to decide whether
to stop or continue the screening pathway toward
a diagnosis
(Fig.3)
.
The HAS
emphasises
that this catch
-
up test should be performed as soon as possible
after
identifying
an unscreened newborn. To ensure continuity of the screening pathway and opti-
mise care, this test should be performed before the corrected age of one month.
Healthcare
professionals must communicate
inconclusive
(“refer”)
results from the second or
catch
-
up screening phase without delay
to ensure that the diagnostic process is initiated.
Diagnostic phase
Infants with an inconclusive
result
(“refer
”) from
the second
-
phase test or the catch
-
up test (on
both ears) should be referred to the diagnostic phase.
This diagnostic phase should be per-
formed to confirm or rule out hearing loss, ideally at one month and before the corrected age
of three months. The diagnosis should be
confirmed or ruled out by a multidisciplinary team
,
composed
of
an ENT physician,
a
Speech and language therapist
,
and a psychologist.
When unilateral or bilateral hearing loss is suspected following screening
at
the facility, the
HAS recommends
investigating
the presence of congenital CMV infection, in accordance with
the recommendations issued by the
Haut Conseil de Santé publique
(HCSP
-
French
High
Council for Public Health) on 18
th
November 2018
1
.
Regular monitoring of the child is also
recommended
to
detect any progression to bilateral hearing loss.
Training and information
The HAS recommends:
‒
R
einforcing training for all healthcare professionals involved in newborn hearing screen-
ing. This training should cover both the technical aspects (
such as the
use of OAE and
AABR tests) and
the
relational aspects,
particularly
how to
delive
r
the
information to
families.
‒
The initial
information
about
this screening
should be
provided
to parents during preg-
nancy, at third
-
trimester antenatal consultations.
‒
Educational
materials should be
developed
and
adapted to the different publics, includ-
ing parents and future parents,
as well
as
healthcare professionals involved in newborn
hearing
loss
screening
and its management.
‒
Families should
be involved throughout the screening pathway, from the initial screen-
ing phase
to diagnosis and treatment.
‒
Families should have
access to
support from a specialised multidisciplinary team in
cases
of
inconclusive
(“refer”)
results
or needs expressed by parents or identified by
1
https://www.hcsp.fr/explore.cgi/avisrapportsdomaine?clefr=1367

HAS • Assessment of the national newborn screening programme for permanent hearing loss.  • juin 2025 8
perinatal professionals. It is important to present the test sequence as an overall screen-
ing process
, providing families
with a clear understanding
of the
continuum
of care
to
limit parental anxiety.
To this end, the HAS highlights the existence of the national hearing loss information centre
(CNIS) dedicated to informing and supporting families throughout the screening, diagnosis and
care phases.
Refusal to participate in the screening programme
If
the parents or holders of parental authority refuse screening
at birth
, the HAS recom-
mends
healthcare professionals
to record
“not screened at the request of the
parents/holders
of parental authority” in the child’s health record. Families may subsequently change their
minds.
Programme management
The HAS recommends
implementing
management and monitoring of the national screening
programme for newborn PBHL, as is done for the national programme for newborn blood spot
screening.
To effectively
address all
aspects
related
to
the methods, organisation, monitoring
and evaluation of the specifications
as agreed
at
the national (steering) level,
following
these
recommendations, the latter should include:
‒
Updating
the screening tests used during the first screening phase (T1 and T2).
‒
Targeted funding of screening in healthcare facilities
through
dedicated budget lines.
‒
Incorporating
the second screening phase (T3) using an AABR test, performed by a
professional listed by the regional operator and its funding arrangements.
‒
Adding
the catch
-
up phase using an AABR test, and its funding arrangements, as well
as the
harmonisation of training and information for
professionals, subject to specific
funding.
‒
A national monitoring system to
collect
data
during
the
assess
ment of
the
program
me
’s
implementation, effectiveness, efficiency and long
-
term impact
.
Technical means and resources
The HAS recommends that sufficient human, material and financial resources be made avail-
able
for this screening,
as well as for
follow
-
up, data feedback and assessment.
The HAS
highlights
the need to develop a calibration guide for newborn hearing screening
devices, which has not been produced to date.
Programme indicators and follow
-
up
The HAS
emphasises
the importance of collecting the indicators proposed by
Santé publique
France
2
to assess
the impact of the screening programme.
2
Screening indicators
: Annual rate of infants covered by the programme, annual rate of screening completeness, an-
nual screening rate among infants covered by the programme, annual refusal rate, rate of infants with suspected PBHL
among infants screened, rate of infants with su
spected hearing loss on the basis of tests carried out in maternity units,
rate of infants having missed screening.
Diagnosis indicators:
Efficacy of follow
-
up of infants with suspected PBHL, rate of moderate PBHL, rate of severe
PBHL, rate of profound PBHL, rate of moderate PBHL among infants screened, rate of severe PBHL among infants
screened, rate of profound PBHL among infants screened
, proportion of infants with a diagnosis of moderate, severe
and profound PBHL (before the age of one year, before six months and before three months).

HAS • Assessment of the national newborn screening programme for permanent hearing loss.  • juin 2025 9
The HAS recommends that the indicators be validated by the programme’s governance
(screening, diagnosis and care process), incorporating those previously proposed by
SpF
, and
adding the following indicators:
1.
Efficacy of the first and second
phases
of screening
‒
Rate of infants with suspected hearing loss according to the tests performed during the
first phase of screening (OAE and AABR or two AABR
tests).
‒
Rate of infants referred to the second screening phase among those initially tested.
‒
Rate of infants
screened during the second phase.
‒
Rate of suspected hearing loss confirmed at the end of the second phase.
2.
Catch
-
up test follow
-
up:
the following indicators should be collected, specifying the rea-
sons why this catch
-
up was performed (
such as
newborns
not
screened and/or born in an
atypical context, such as
home birth, birth centre or early discharge from the maternity unit
after less than 48 hours)
.
‒
Rate of infants
who have
undergone
a catch
-
up test.
‒
Rate of suspected hearing loss identified in this phase.
3.
Diagnostic phase.
‒
Rate of infants with confirmed bilateral hearing loss, specifying the history of the tests
previously performed (T1, T2, T3) or catch
-
up test
(home birth, birth centre or early
discharge from the maternity unit after less than 48 hours).
‒
Average age at the start of the diagnostic phase, according to hearing loss severity
(mild, moderate, severe, profound) and the type of hearing loss (unilateral and bilateral).
‒
 Care (Treatment).
‒
Average age of initiation of care,
especially in case
s
of
profound hearing loss
.
Outlook
The HAS recommends collecting French data on:
‒
The cost of hearing loss.
‒
The cost
-
effectiveness of interventions.
‒
The acceptability of the screening strategies implemented (to families and healthcare
professionals).
Due to the
lack
of data on
the
clinical utility and the impact of
including
newborn screening for
unilateral hearing loss, the HAS highlights the
importance
of assessing
management in regions
where this screening is
already
in place before including it in the national screening pro-
gramme.
These recommendations may be updated
as new
epidemiological and scientific
evidence
emerges
.

HAS • Assessment of the national newborn screening programme for permanent hearing loss.  • juin 2025 10

1
.

*
Hearing confirmed

Refer: inconclusive

2
.

conclusive

3
.

-

phase
